# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Or you can fork this repository and set your own key words in `main.py`:

Last update: 2026-10-14

👍Thanks to [zezhishao/DailyArXiv](https://github.com/zezhishao/DailyArXiv) and [Cool Paper](https://papers.cool).

| **[Measuring Harness-Induced Belief Divergence in Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.04528v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software agents are becoming increasingly important in modern coding workflows, where they solve complex tasks through multi-step interaction with code repositories. A central component of these systems is the execution harness, which governs how the underlying LLM observes, acts on, and verifies its environment. We show that the harness can in turn shape the agent's evolving beliefs about the environment, even when the task, environment, and base LLM remain fixed. To reveal this effect, we introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how an agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under alternative harnesses. We further separate immediate divergence introduced by the interface from divergence that develops over longer horizons. Across controlled coding tasks include HIBench-Code, SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench, we find substantial cross-harness belief divergence that is often invisible from terminal success alone and can propagate into different downstream actions. Building on this observation, we introduce \textbf{Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM)}, which exposes evidence hidden or compressed by harness execution and aligns the resulting belief trajectories across different harnesses. Our findings reveal that harness design can systematically shape how software agents understand and act on their environments, providing practical guidance for building and evaluating more reliable coding agents.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[P-PAS: Prefill-Pressure Adaptive Scheduling for Long-Context LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15171v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.15171v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-context LLM applications such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and agentic systems often process tens of thousands of input tokens to produce short outputs, making end-to-end request latency an important serving objective. We show that the maximum number of batched tokens (MBT), which controls the token scheduling budget in vLLM, has a scheduling-pressure-dependent effect on latency. Larger token budgets can reduce latency under low scheduling pressure, while smaller budgets become preferable under higher pressure. Consequently, no single static MBT performs best across load regimes. We introduce Prefill-Pressure Adaptive Scheduling (P-PAS), a lightweight policy that dynamically adapts the global scheduling budget using concurrent prefill and decode state. P-PAS retains a large token budget under low pressure and switches to a smaller budget when multiple prefills compete with active decoding. Across models, workload configurations, and GPUs, P-PAS maintains low end-to-end latency across changing load regimes. Kernel-level profiling shows that the crossover arises from a trade-off between large-chunk prefill efficiency and prefill--decode interference. We further extend the pressure signal to heterogeneous request shapes with P-PAS+. On mixed-shape workloads using Qwen3.8-27B and Nemotron-3.5-Lightning-30B-A3B, P-PAS+ reduces mean end-to-end latency by 3.0--3.1% relative to the best tested static token budget. Across exactly matched logical requests, 91.2% of Qwen requests and 88.5% of Nemotron requests complete faster with P-PAS+. Code and artifacts are available at https://github.com/TimoSaemann/ppas-vllm .</p></details> |  |
| **[QTestBench: A Test Framework for Evaluating LLM-Generated Quantum Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13811v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13811v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong results in classical code generation and are increasingly used to write the unit tests that safeguard that code. Whether they can do the same for quantum software is less clear, because quantum programs are probabilistic, depend on numerical tolerances, and are judged by domain-specific checkers, so a well-formed test can still disagree with the benchmark's verdict. Existing quantum benchmarks measure whether a model produces a correct solution, but not whether its tests can distinguish correct implementations from faulty ones. We introduce QTestBench, a framework that evaluates LLM-generated quantum test suites against independent benchmark checkers, never treating the tests themselves as ground truth. For each task, QTestBench prompts models to produce syntactic, semantic, and behavioral tests, injects candidate solutions at runtime, and compares each test verdict with the checker's label under three validation cohorts, each answering a distinct diagnostic question. We benchmark five LLMs on eight PennyLane QHack challenges and ten Qiskit HumanEval tasks. Generated suites almost always execute, but discrimination lags behind: the best generator reaches a test quality of 0.71 on QHack and 0.34 on Qiskit HumanEval, so executability alone overstates test quality. Oracle-verified synthetic mutants stabilize bug-detection estimates when natural faulty solutions are scarce, as on three of ten Qiskit tasks that yield none. QTestBench offers a reproducible, cross-framework benchmark for the trustworthiness of LLM-generated quantum tests.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 2026 IEEE 2nd International Conference on Quantum Artificial Intelligence (QAI), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Tracing and Taming Hallucinated Terms via Subsequence-Association Filtering and Breadth-Aware Selection for LLM Query Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13810v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13810v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are now a standard source of query expansion terms but they hallucinate. They inject non existent entities and off-topic terms that add ranking noise and can hurt strong retrievers. Existing remedies such as corpus grounding, mutual verification and preference alignment treat hallucinated terms as an undifferentiated nuisance to be filtered by agreement heuristics, without a mechanistic account of why a given term appears or a principled criterion for how many terms to keep. We propose Subsequence-Association Filtered, breadth-aware query expansion (SAFE-QE), a training-free, retriever agnostic method that draws on the subsequence-association account of hallucination. We score each candidate expansion term by its corpus conditioned cross-context reproducibility, terms that reappear under randomised query contexts yet lack support in the pseudo-relevant set are flagged as products of dominant hallucinatory associations and down-weighted. Drawing on the characterisation of language generation as a trade-off between consistency (no hallucination) and breadth (coverage), we cast term selection as constrained coverage maximisation over the pseudo-relevant set, keeping a set that is approximately broad but consistent and stable across resampling. We also introduce the Hallucinated Expansion Term Rate, a query side faithfulness metric that counts injected expansion terms no relevant document supports. We evaluate our approach on benchmarked TREC and BEIR collections. Our SAFE-QE is statistically indistinguishable from generative and corpus-steered expansion on nDCG@10 while injecting fewer unsupported terms 4.3 per query against 27.8 for Query2doc and 57.5 for HyDE and on a strong learned sparse retriever, where every expansion method regresses. Faithfulness is therefore available at no measurable cost in ranking quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeltaLLM: Delta-Key Sparse Attention for Edge LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19608v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2507.19608v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-device LLM inference is increasingly important for edge platforms but remains challenging due to high computational and memory demands and tight resource constraints. Modern applications often involve long prompts. Although compact models reduce linear-layer costs through weight compression, attention remains largely unoptimized and becomes the dominant bottleneck, especially during prefilling. Existing attention pruning methods primarily target highly parallel hardware (e.g., GPUs) and long context windows, limiting their suitability for edge scenarios. We present DeltaLLM, a training-free LLM framework based on algorithm-hardware-dataflow co-design to accelerate prefilling attention. DeltaLLM introduces a causal-attention-aware K-only delta formulation that enables Delta-Key reuse across query heads under Grouped-Query Attention (GQA). A context-aware hybrid strategy preserves exact attention near the diagonal while applying delta approximation to nonlocal regions. For score-value multiplication, we propose a vectorized structured sparse online softmax (VSSOS) that converts fine-grained spatial score sparsity into hardware-friendly column-level skipping. A specialized vector-processor architecture and unified sparse/dense attention dataflow exploit the resulting sparsity. Evaluated on BitNet-b1.58-2B-4T and LLaMA3.2-1B-Instruct, DeltaLLM achieves up to 60% Delta-Key sparsity and 63% structured attention-score sparsity with negligible average accuracy loss. On the KV260 FPGA board, it delivers a 1.62x speedup over TeLLMe-v2 while reducing DSP and BRAM usage. These results show that DeltaLLM supports efficient edge LLM deployment and integration with existing pipelines without fine-tuning.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This paper has been accepted by IEEE Transactions on Circuits and Systems for Artificial Intelligence (TCASAI)</p></details> |
| **[Inject a Turn, Not a Token: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13672v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning. Unlike prior training-based approaches that fine-tune an LLM to generate revisions or feedback, we learn where to intervene in failed rollouts and determine how to redirect them. To choose where to intervene, we train a 130K-parameter MLP head on a frozen encoder using only final-answer correctness. For redirection, we find that under a fixed generation budget, the tested inline injections fail to reliably redirect reasoning, whereas presenting the same instruction in a new user turn changes the model's course. The resulting correction framework, UPTURN (User-Prompt TURN injection at a learned position), requires no LLM fine-tuning: it retains a selected prefix, injects an instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix. Across four reasoners and six math benchmarks, UPTURN improves average accuracy in every setting over a 32-rollout baseline. It outperforms self-correction and reflection-token injection baselines under matched correction budgets, transfers across reasoners and to GPQA-Diamond without retraining, and complements GRPO and self-correction methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Multi-Agent LLM System for Automated Tuning of Specialized Neural Network Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13663v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13663v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many machine learning approaches are two-level: an expert selects a class of methods, within this class the choice of key components is critical and usually manual. This paper addresses the automatic tuning of these components and proposes a framework transferable across models of different nature. The multi-agent large language model (LLM) framework has three roles---analyst, coder, and recorder~---with an experiment log and a sandboxed execution environment. Unlike systems that optimize the entire ML script or search architectures in a discrete space, the framework operates over an open but semantically narrow space: the agent generates code only for the specified components, leaving the overall network structure unchanged. The approach is validated in two directions: tuning a classification spiking neural network with correlation learning, and solving high-order differential equations with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN). Across five tasks---spiking classification on MNIST and Digits, and boundary-value problems for the generalized nonlinear Schrödinger equation (GNLSE) and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky (KS) equations---the agent outperforms the baselines: classification accuracy is 13 percentage points (MNIST) and 6 percentage points (Digits) higher; the error against the analytical single-soliton solution is reduced approximately 2491-fold; the invariant-preservation error drops approximately 325-fold in the selected GNLSE run with a Gaussian perturbation and by more than 100-fold on average over repeated initializations; and the mass drift drops 14-fold for the KS problem with a Gaussian perturbation. The framework thus tunes key components on tasks of different nature without changing its internal structure.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Critical Look at Power Sampling when Test Time Sharpening LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13653v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13653v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved LLM reasoning, yet growing evidence suggests it mainly performs distribution sharpening: concentrating probability mass on reasoning paths the base model already produces. Recent work therefore seeks RLVR's gains without needing reward signals, by sampling from the power distribution $π_α\propto π_{\text{base}}^α$ via Markov chain Monte Carlo or sequential Monte Carlo. We show that these samplers fail to converge to $π_α$ under realistic compute budgets, and that their gains are largely recreated via simple Best-of-$N$ with appropriate grading . This suggests they succeed by selecting high-probability sequences, not by approximating $π_α$, raising a natural question: is the power distribution itself a worthwhile target? We test this hypothesis directly by fine-tuning the base model to match $π_α$, sidestepping sampler convergence issues entirely. Despite approximating the target far more faithfully than existing samplers, this strategy yields gains that are noisy and specific to certain models and sampling tasks. Together, our results suggest that the value of power sampling lies not in faithfully targeting $π_α$, but in the simpler goal of extracting sequences the base model already deems likely.</p></details> |  |
| **[ToFu: Exploiting and Fuzzing the LLM-Orchestrator-Tool Trust Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13651v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13651v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that interact with external software through tools, with an orchestration layer translating model outputs into structured tool invocations. This architecture creates an LLM-orchestrator-tool trust boundary where tool semantics may go unenforced and LLM-generated arguments may be incorrectly treated as trusted. We present ToFu, a tool-aware framework that automatically derives semantic security checks for LLM tools from their existing metadata and a CWE-guided sanitizer corpus. ToFu specializes these checks to individual tools and automatically generates fuzzing harnesses and semantic oracles using runtime introspection and outcome sanitization. We evaluated ToFu across 273 MCP servers containing 3405 tools and identified verified bugs corresponding to CWE weakness classes in 343 tools across 118 of the servers. Our findings show that real-world tools frequently fail to enforce their documented input semantics, making it unsafe to treat LLM-generated arguments as trusted.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 4 figures, 16 tables. Evaluates 273 open-source MCP servers (3405 tools); 253 verified semantic security bugs in 118 servers. Appendices include a case study, a Repeat-After-Me transcript on LangGraph, the full sanitizer corpus, the list of evaluated repositories, and the verbatim LLM prompts</p></details> |
| **[SPARQL-LLM in the TEXT2SPARQL 2026 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13648v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13648v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Querying distributed data represented as Knowledge Graphs (KGs) via SPARQL remains a difficult task, requiring fluency in query syntax, endpoint specifications, and schema details. Knowledge Graph Question Answering (KGQA) systems address this by automatically translating natural language into SPARQL, and recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have made this translation increasingly feasible. In this paper, we describe SPARQL-LLM, our open-source KGQA system, in the context of the TEXT2SPARQL 2026 challenge. In this challenge, SPARQL-LLM ranks first overall among eight competing approaches across all datasets, achieving up to 59% higher accuracy than the second-best approach and supporting multilingual question answering in English, German, and Spanish. SPARQL-LLM is publicly released at https://github.com/sib-swiss/sparql-llm and deployed in production over decentralized bioinformatics KGs at https://www.expasy.org/chat.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 1 figure, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[AMLworld-Compact: Importance-Weighted Downsampling for Cost-Effective LLM Evaluation and Error Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13627v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13627v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating large language models (LLMs) on AMLworld's smallest test splits is costly because each of over a million targets requires a separate graph prompt. Sampling reduces this cost, but small balanced subsets change unweighted precision and F1. We introduce AMLworld-Compact, an evaluation set that retains every illicit test transaction and samples benign transactions from supervised-score strata. Its HT-Coreset protocol reduces evaluation targets by 271x and 611x on HI-Small and LI-Small. Horvitz-Thompson weights give unbiased full-test confusion-count estimates for fixed predictions; recall is exact, while precision and F1 are ratio estimates. In a held-out study using Graph Feature Preprocessor (GFP) features, one boosted-tree family defines strata for evaluating the other over 500 draws per configuration. At the released sizes, F1 RMSE is 0.00-2.58 percentage points versus 4.44-7.41 for uniform sampling with HT weights; conservative intervals remain broad. Under the evaluated graph prompts, seven open-weight LLMs have weighted precision near the predict-all-illicit baseline. In an outcome-stratified audit of 1,000 HI-Small few-shot outputs from one run per model, lexical checks pass for 84.5% of correct and 56.2% of incorrect binary verdicts, so these markers do not establish correctness. We release the evaluation sets, construction code, and audit tools at https://huggingface.co/datasets/typhoon-ai/AMLworldCompactEval and https://github.com/nat-nischw/AMLworld-Compact.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 5 figures, 27 tables. Under review</p></details> |
| **[SWE-DeceptionBench: Benchmarking Deceptive Behaviors of LLMs Across the Software Development Lifecycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13626v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13626v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used throughout the software development lifecycle. This growing reliance on LLMs raises concerns about their trustworthiness, with deception posing a particularly important risk. Deceptive behavior takes the form of misleading responses or assistance in fabricating, concealing, or distorting information. To study this behavior, we introduce SWE-DeceptionBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLM deception in software engineering. The benchmark covers six lifecycle stages and two deception triggers: Active Deception, where models recognize infeasible or conflicting requirements but still present them as achievable, and Passive Deception, where models help fulfill explicit user requests to mislead others. We further introduce Neutral, Reward, and Pressure conditions to study the effect of external inducements, resulting in 720 test instances in Chinese and English. Evaluating seven mainstream LLMs, we find substantial deception even under the Neutral condition, with aggregate rates of 49.88% for Active Deception and 64.29% for Passive Deception. Deception rates vary across lifecycle stages, and external inducements affect Active and Passive Deception to different extents. We also evaluate lightweight defenses, finding that guard models miss many deceptive requests, and Self-Reminder reduces but does not eliminate deceptive responses. These findings highlight the need for more effective deception detection and mitigation methods to support trustworthy LLM-based software engineering.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoDrive: Pareto Evolution for Safety-Critical Autonomous Driving via Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03678v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.03678v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating safety-critical scenarios is essential for validating and improving autonomous driving systems, yet it inherently requires maximizing adversariality to expose failures while preserving realism. Existing methods usually manage this trade-off with handcrafted heuristics, confining generation to known priors and overlooking underexplored patterns. While recent open-ended agentic evolution can push this limit, unconstrained general agents lack strict simulator grounding and tend to collapse the multi-objective tension into single-scalar maximization. Here we present EvoDrive, the first automated, LLM-based agentic evolution framework for multi-objective scenario generation. EvoDrive employs a simulator-grounded actor-critic architecture where a memory-driven actor iteratively proposes improvements to the generators and critics filter out implausible candidates, and a self-evolving world evaluator routes promising proposals to optimize simulation budgets. EvoDrive further maintains a Pareto archive of evaluated candidates to preserve diverse attack-realism trade-offs and guide future evolution via simulation feedback. Benchmark results on MetaDrive and CARLA show that EvoDrive not only significantly expands the Pareto frontier across various generators, but also produces valuable scenarios for policy training.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026)</p></details> |
| **[AudioTETRIS: Token Elimination via Transitional Importance Signals for Efficient Audio LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13600v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13600v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) achieve strong performance on speech-text tasks, but standard audio encoders produce hundreds of tokens for even short audio clips. Because self-attention scales quadratically in sequence length, this inflates inference-time compute substantially. Current training-free token compression methods prune or merge tokens based solely on coarse attention or feature similarity. However, treating audio tokens as generic, isotropic features smooths over spectral transitions, destroying fine-grained phonetic transitions essential for decoding speech. In contrast, speech signals exhibit highly structured temporal patterns where information density peaks at acoustic transitions. We propose AudioTETRIS, a training-free, two-stage framework that uses the audio encoder's representation geometry as an acoustic prior for token importance before applying attention-based compression. Instead of discarding encoder structure after the encoding stage, AudioTETRIS propagates this prior directly into the LLM decoder to compute a per-token fused score. This score drives progressive top-$k$ token selection across decoder layers without requiring additional training or auxiliary models. Evaluated on Qwen2-Audio-7B, Phi-4-Multimodal, and Audio Flamingo 3, AudioTETRIS retains near-uncompressed performance on several speech tasks using only 25% of audio tokens, matches or surpasses full-token accuracy at moderate compression rates, and consistently outperforms existing training-free baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Personalization Without Stereotyping: Do Speech LLMs Know When to Turn a Deaf Ear?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13550v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13550v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent speech LLMs can leverage rich paralinguistic cues, enabling more complex and personalized interactions. However, little work investigates how this sensitivity can lead to stereotyping, and existing evaluations largely measure demographic disparities without distinguishing beneficial personalization from stereotypical behavior. We introduce BASIS-Bench, a benchmark designed to disentangle these two aspects through three axes: (1) demographic robustness, measuring disparities in content-based tasks across speakers and demographic classification capabilities; (2) stereotypical bias, measuring whether perceived demographic cues trigger unprompted stereotypical behavior; and (3) personalization, measuring whether models can tailor responses to demographic characteristics when explicitly requested. We evaluate eight recent speech LLMs and find that, despite often recognizing demographic cues, most fail to translate them into appropriate personalization. Explicit demographic labels improve personalization but also amplify stereotypical associations already present in the textual backbones. These findings highlight the need to train speech LLMs not only to perceive demographic cues, but to learn when they should shape their responses.</p></details> |  |
| **[Phonology-Aware Speaker Extraction Frontend for Audio LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13500v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13500v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Target speaker extraction (TSE) is a key frontend for enabling audio large language models (LLMs) to recognize and understand a designated speaker in overlapping speech. However, multilingual mixtures exhibit heterogeneous extraction demands, causing fixed-depth systems to either under-process challenging inputs or waste computation on inputs that have already converged. Existing TSE frontends generally apply uniform computation, while Audio-LLM adaptation methods neither explicitly model language-conditioned extraction demands nor preserve plug-and-play compatibility with frozen downstream models. We therefore propose LATTE, a lightweight 5.2M-parameter frontend that combines an acoustic Difficulty Estimator, a task-adaptive phonology-informed prior learned from multi-depth TSE gains, and an Adaptive Hourglass Extractor to route each mixture without requiring language identity at inference. On PolyExtract, LATTE achieves a macro SI-SNRi of 15.07 dB at 0.30 GFLOPs, retaining performance within 0.09 dB of a 0.53-GFLOP fixed-depth model while reducing computation by 43.4%, and lowers the average WER from 16.8% to 14.6% relative to TF-GridNet. By jointly modeling sample-level extraction demands and phonological structure, LATTE provides a practical framework for quality-aware, compute-efficient multilingual TSE and offers a scalable path toward robust Audio-LLM deployment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Polarization Dynamics under Algorithmic Recommendations in LLM-based Online Social Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13351v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13351v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do recommendation algorithms contribute to the formation of polarized opinions in online social networks? This question has been studied through empirical analyses of real platforms and mathematical models of opinion dynamics. Empirical studies capture user behavior but are often limited by noisy, incomplete, or inaccessible platform data, while mathematical models offer control and interpretability but usually rely on simplified opinion update rules. More recently, large language model (LLM)-based social simulations have provided an alternative for studying recommendation and opinion dynamics through natural-language interactions. However, how different ways of applying recommendations shape opinion dynamics remains underexplored. In this work, we provide a controlled comparison of two different recommendation mechanisms in an LLM-based online social network simulation. Edge Recommendation (ER) introduces new connections in the network, while Content-Exposure Recommendation (CR) exposes additional users to individual posts without modifying the underlying graph. Our framework represents users as LLM-based agents who interact in natural language while maintaining evolving opinion states. We measure polarization as opinion dispersion, extremization as the average distance of opinions from the neutral midpoint, and reinforcement as interactions that amplify already aligned opinions. Our results show higher mean polarization and extremization across recommendation conditions compared with the no-recommendation baseline, with absolute differences of up to $0.12$ in our main experiments.</p></details> | 18 pages, 10 figures |
| **[From the Fluency Fallacy to the Micro-to-Macro Validity Gap: Opportunities and Pitfalls of LLMs in Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19364v4)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2507.19364v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into social simulation has generated enthusiasm but also raises methodological and epistemological challenges. This critical review examines LLMs as cognitive or decision-making components of simulated agents from a computational social science perspective. We argue that their behavioral and epistemic limitations can become systemic vulnerabilities when scaled to multi-agent societies. We map the evolving landscape of LLM-driven platforms, from narrative sandboxes to population-scale simulations, and develop a framework for analyzing the Micro-to-Macro Validity Gap: the propagation, amplification, or contraction of micro-level limitations-including hallucinations, stochastic inconsistency, representational biases, and alignment effects-into agent-level distortions (e.g., persona convergence) and macro-level risks (e.g., artificial consensus and behavioral homogenization). We also highlight evaluative vulnerabilities such as the Fluency Fallacy and automation bias. We identify contexts where LLM-based agents offer operational value, including serious games, participatory environments, and exploratory modeling, distinguishing these uses from confirmatory research with unconstrained models and precise social forecasting. Finally, we examine theory-driven hybrid architectures embedding LLMs in explicit, mechanistic, and reproducible Agent-Based Modeling (ABM) frameworks. While promising, these architectures do not guarantee epistemic control: validity requires multi-level evaluation of environmental dynamics, individual behavior, cross-level interactions, and aggregate outcomes. They also remain vulnerable to physics washing.</p></details> |  |
| **[Host-Aware Web Crawling for LLM Pretraining Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13321v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13321v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web crawling is a major source of large language model (LLM) pretraining data, yet data-selection pipelines discard a large share of crawled pages. We investigate how a crawler should allocate its budget across and within hosts to collect more high-quality pretraining data. Combining document-level quality classifier scores, web-graph centrality metrics, and Common Crawl's per-host crawl coverage, we find that roughly half of the variation in classifier scores occurs between hosts. In the crawls we analyze, a few pages from a host also provide a useful estimate of the quality of its remaining pages. Common Crawl uses harmonic centrality, a graph centrality metric, to guide its crawl allocation. In our simulations, using centrality to choose which hosts to crawl yields limited gains over random ordering and performs worse for some classifiers. For deciding how deeply to crawl each host, sampling a few pages and then fetching more from hosts with higher-scoring samples collects several times more high-quality pages per fetch than uniform depth or centrality-based allocation. Finally, Common Crawl's observed per-host coverage has little correlation with the evaluated quality signals. We estimate that tens of millions of high-quality pages remain among known but uncrawled URLs in each crawl examined, with most concentrated on a small share of hosts.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at WaC-13 @ EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Conditional Vendi Score: Prompt-Aware Diversity Evaluation for Generative AI Models and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02817v3)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2411.02817v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative models guided by text prompts are widely evaluated for fidelity and prompt alignment, yet their ability to produce diverse outputs remains underexplored. Existing diversity metrics such as Vendi and RKE, which are based on the von Neumann and Rényi entropies of kernel matrices, were developed for unconditional models and cannot distinguish prompt-induced from model-induced variability. We address this gap by introducing \textit{Conditional-Vendi} and \textit{Conditional-RKE}, diversity measures derived from the conditional entropy of positive semidefinite matrices. These scores isolate model-induced diversity in prompt-guided generation, with Conditional-RKE enjoying an $O(1/\sqrt{n})$ convergence rate. For Conditional-Vendi, we introduce a truncated-spectrum approximation that yields scalable and consistent estimates. | **[Agreement Without Alignment: Label-Free Rubric Clarification Increases LLM-Judge Consensus but Not Agreement with Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13303v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13303v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges are increasingly deployed as panels, and disagreement among panel members is often read as a symptom of an unclear rubric. | **[PreSTO: Predictive Subtree Prefetching for Fast LLM Power Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13171v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13171v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Power sampling improves large language model (LLM) reasoning at test time by sampling from a sharpened version of the base model's distribution. | **[Constitutional Gating and Deterministic Recovery for Multi-Agent LLM Negotiation: Ablations Against a Stateful Adversarial Gatekeeper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11542v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11542v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems negotiating with a stateful counterpart waste model calls in three ways: polite loops that never meet the counterpart's hidden acceptance condition, malformed outputs that trigger retries, and compliance deadlocks in which the counterpart demands something the agent must refuse. | **[GeoFitBench-LLM: A Benchmark for Assessing Large Language Models' Capabilities in Geospatial Data Requirements Elicitation for Geo-Analytic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12669v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12669v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are becoming central to GeoAI systems, yet their evaluation rarely considers the elicitation of geospatial data requirements. | **[SynAct: Synergistic Active Feedback Learning for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12651v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12651v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents executing multi-step workflows fail in two fundamentally different ways: recoverable slips, which further attempts can repair, and knowledge gaps, which retrying cannot resolve. | **[Knowing Is Not Telling: Identity Ossification in LLM Representations of Trans-border Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12610v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12610v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ethnic and cultural identities rarely align with political borders, a tension made sharper by migration and long-standing cross-border ties. It appears in only 10.1% of GPT-5.1's English answers, but in 62.6% of DeepSeek-V3.2's Chinese answers. | **[Encoder Awakening via Adapters: Effective Domain-Adaptive Fine-tuning of Speech-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17981v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.17981v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speech Large Language Models (Speech-LLMs), typically built from a pre-trained speech encoder, a modality projector, and an LLM fine-tuned with Low-Rank Adapters (LoRA), have shown strong Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) performance on general-domain speech. | **[Long Text to Predictive Features: LLM-Guided Blockwise Feature Engineering via Executable Program Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12390v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12390v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial risk-control systems typically rely on structured-data models for efficient prediction, yet substantial valuable information remains embedded in unstructured long text. | **[OnTrack: Real-Time Monitoring and Intervention in LLM Agent Trajectories via Streaming Structure-Aware Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12375v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12375v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents are deployed in applications from trip planners and stock trading to IT incident triage. | **[LLM Persona Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39882v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.39882v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-training equips large language models (LLMs) with a broad repertoire of behavioral patterns associated with roles, styles, values, and goals. | **[SparseDecoding: Decoding-Aware Pruning for Accurate and Efficient LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12327v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12327v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The memory-bound nature of the decoding stage of large language model (LLM) inference incurs significant latency. The Hessian calculated on the natural sequence is different from that calculated on the generated sequence. We observe that this discrepancy causes the activation distribution during generation to deviate from that used for pruning, further hurting the pruned model performance. Moreover, most existing LLM pruning methods that bring actual speedup primarily target the sparse matrix-matrix (SpMM) multiplication, providing limited support for the sparse matrix-vector (SpMV) operations, which dominate decoding. Specifically, at the algorithmic axis, SparseDecoding constructs calibration matrices from layer-wise activations collected during the dense-model autoregressive generation, excluding prefill, thereby aligning the pruning objective with the decoding activations. | **[Prior or Feedback? What an LLM Uses When Adapting Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12325v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12325v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Do LLM scientific agents rely only on their initial task context, or do they adapt their decisions in response to experimental feedback? | **[Looking Inside LLMs: Small-World Connectivity as a Signature of Reasoning Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12304v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12304v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding large language model (LLM) reasoning requires looking beyond behavioral performance to examine how reasoning ability is reflected in internal organization. These observations motivate the hypothesis that high core and low bridge scores serve as structural indicators of head importance for reasoning capability. | **[ROMA: LLM System for Real-World Object-Centric Multi-Sensory Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06955v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.06955v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans inherently understand the physical world through an active process. When sensory evidence is insufficient to infer physical properties, we naturally interact with the environment by deciding what information is missing, how to acquire it, and when sufficient evidence has been obtained. ROMA integrates vision, audio, tactile, and force sensing into a reasoning-interaction-feedback loop. | **[TokenRouter: Efficient Serving System for Token-Level LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12242v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12242v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) routing distributes inference work across different models, advancing the cost-quality Pareto frontier of LLM serving. | **[Beyond Euclidean Clipping: Overcoming Exploration Collapse in LLM RL via Riemannian Isometric Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10169v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.10169v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. | **[Poster: A Preliminary Study of LLM Distillation Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12137v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12137v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unauthorized model distillation, in which a model is trained on the outputs of a proprietary large language model (LLM), is a growing threat to model providers. | **[Universal Textual Teaching for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12114v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12114v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge distillation (KD) transfers knowledge from stronger Teacher models to weaker Student models, but most methods require training the Student parameters, thereby binding the distilled knowledge to a specific architecture and checkpoint. | **[All Verdicts are Not Equal: Rethinking LLM Judge Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12083v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12083v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-as-a-Judge is the standard paradigm for NLP evaluation, yet its systemic reliability remains poorly understood despite being widely treated as a deterministic ground truth. | **[Just for FUNS: LLM-Guided Spatio-Temporal Graph Node Generation for Forecasting Unobserved Node States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08818v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08818v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatio-temporal forecasting is a cornerstone of logistics, urban planning, and intelligent transportation systems. | **[Natural Language to First-Order Logic LLM-based Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12030v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12030v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have renewed interest in autoformalization. | **[How Do LLMs Change Predictions Under Negation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09571v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09571v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Negation is an essential feature of human language, yet large language models (LLMs) remain unreliable in processing it. | **[Learn2Play Bench: How Well Do LLM Agents Learn from Experience in Unfamiliar Environments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08215v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08215v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning from experience is essential for LLM agents to adapt to unfamiliar and dynmaic environments. | **[Multi-Task Evolution for Zero-Shot Cross-Problem Generalization using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03316v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.03316v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing effective heuristics for diverse combinatorial optimization problems requires substantial expertise and repeated search. | **[Can LLMs Fix It Without Code? | **[Structural and Representational Agency in LLM-Driven Timbre Generation and Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12532v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12532v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative creative interfaces routinely report the user's position within a space of possibilities. The first is exactly characterisable: the reachable set is a zonotope whose dimension increases by one per sound placed, so the space the model may act within is demonstrably the user's own. The second is inaccurate. The displayed position differs from measurement by approximately three times the width of that region, and the error has two independent sources: model-predicted coordinates, whose accuracy varies twofold between the two models evaluated, and the interpolation step, which arises from combining synthesiser parameters and is unaffected by model quality. End to end, 26 of 36 refinement instructions produced no perceptible change, and under the weaker model the facility performed no action while reporting convergence. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Conference, Creative AI Track)</p></details> |
| **[Anytime-valid detection of LLM weight exfiltration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11843v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11843v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A compromised LLM inference server can leak model weights by encoding payload bits in otherwise plausible token choices. Compared with a hard per-token alarm, the e-process combines weak evidence across responses while providing explicit anytime false-alarm control.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for oral presentation at the NeurIPS 2026 workshop E-Values: From Statistics to ML (Paris, December 13 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FlowState: Execution State as Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34565v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.34565v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon tasks require LLM agents to continually draw on information from earlier interactions. | **[On the Risks of using LLM-Generated Tests for Regression Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11835v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11835v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software is under constant evolution: developers continuously add features, fix bugs, and refactor code, and any of these changes may break existing functionality. This is beneficial when the implementation is correct, but problematic when the code contains faults: the generated tests may then encode and preserve incorrect behavior. To investigate this risk, we apply LLM-based regression test generation to pull requests merged into the main branch of software projects and study the impact of the generated tests on subsequent project evolution. We distinguish between fault-revealing tests, which assert correctly implemented behavior, and fault-enforcing tests, which assert faulty behavior. Across 145 pull requests from SciPy, Qiskit, and pandas, 8%-17% of the generated tests are fault-enforcing, while only 2.4%-4.8% reveal faults. Fault-enforcing tests persist over time: after several subsequent commits, 83%-91% of them are still relevant and pass. They also accumulate: when the faults of all pull requests are combined in one codebase, 83%-92% remain enforced at the end of the commit history, and the developer-written test suite detects only 14%-30% of them. Our results reveal a fundamental risk of LLM-generated regression tests: without manual validation, they may encode faulty behavior as expected behavior, allowing bugs to persist across software revisions and largely evade developer-maintained test suites. | **[PlurVA-LLM-2026 Shared Task Track-1: Pluralistic Value Alignment in LLMs via Multilingual Fine-Tuning and Threshold Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32382v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.32382v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present our system for the PlurVA-LLM 2026 Shared Task Track-1, which focuses on pluralistic value alignment in the contexts of China, Indonesia, and Sri Lanka. | **[Narrow and Deep: An Ontology Tower as the Knowledge of an LLM Agent for an Industrial Equipment System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11768v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11768v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are beginning to operate industrial energy equipment, and what they get right depends on what they are told about the plant. | **[Same Outcome, Different Evidence: Intent Recovery in LLM Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11766v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11766v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safety evaluations of large language models commonly summarize harmful-output behavior with attack success rate (ASR). Together, these results show that non-harmful outcomes are not equally informative about model safety, motivating the joint reporting of intent recovery and ASR in LLM safety evaluation. Code and experiment inputs: https://github.com/kevinjiang0121-cyber/IRIS</p></details> |
| **[Thinking Inertia: LLMs Keep Thinking When Told Not To](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11765v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11765v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly ship with explicit "thinking modes", yet their counterpart, "no-thinking", has received far less attention. Website: https://thinking-inertia.github.io GitHub: https://github.com/thinking-inertia/code</p></details> |
| **[MemTrial: Learning When to Trust Memory in LLM Portfolio Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11732v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11732v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents for portfolio management learn from experience: they credit each experience in their memory with the outcome of the decisions that used it. | **[UkrMeme: Multimodal Explainable Persuasion Detection in Ukrainian Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13783v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13783v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memes play a central role in modern online communication, especially in contexts of political conflict where visual and textual cues combine to convey persuasive messages. Finally, we compare Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) and model-generated token attributions and analyze the semantic categories of evidence emphasized by each explanation method.</p></details> |  |
| **[SeoulMMOD: A Large-Scale Multimodal Origin-Destination Flow Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13730v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13730v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Origin-destination (OD) flow forecasting supports urban mobility decisions by predicting the volume and patterns of human movement across a city. With partial mode coverage, observed demand changes are difficult to separate into shifts across transport modes and changes in total travel. We introduce SeoulMMOD, a large-scale public benchmark for citywide multimodal mobility in Seoul. Results show that several spatio-temporal models that work at district level do not scale to sub-district OD forecasting, and that naive joint multi-mode training does not consistently improve accuracy. | **[Can Multimodal Decision Models Classify Breast Ultrasound Images? | **[VaME: Exploring Variational Latent Reasoning for Multimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33402v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.33402v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Universal multimodal retrieval requires compact embeddings that preserve task-relevant semantic information across diverse modalities. | **[HRIL: Learning Multimodal Synergy via Higher-Order Tensor Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12393v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12393v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-supervised multimodal representation learning has achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, yet capturing synergistic information remains challenging due to the complexity of cross-modal interactions. Camera-ready version</p></details> |
| **[Gestalt: Large Multimodal Interplay Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00576v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.00576v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose Gestalt, a new paradigm of large multimodal model built around multimodal interplay. | **[Multimodal Large Language Models as Image Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06578v3)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.06578v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) classification performance depends critically on evaluation protocol and ground truth quality. | **[CoPoE: Multimodal Fusion via Decomposable Disease-Coordinate Product-of-Experts for Missing-Modality Alzheimer's Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11394v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11394v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Alzheimer's disease (AD) diagnosis benefits from integrating heterogeneous clinical, imaging, genomic, and biomarker evidence, but clinical cohorts frequently suffer from irregular modality missingness. | **[HarnessIR: Harnessing Multimodal Foundation Models for Universal Real-World Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10133v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.10133v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world low-quality images suffer from complex mixed degradations, including but not limited to noise, blur, atmospheric effects, etc. Recent agentic methods usually model real-world image restoration (Real-IR) as a sequential tool calling problem over task-specific single-degradation restoration models. | **[ActiveMedAgent: Cost-Aware Trajectory Learning for Multimodal Medical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11140v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.11140v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical diagnosis is inherently sequential: clinicians escalate from cheap to costly tests only when additional evidence is expected to resolve diagnostic uncertainty. | **[DiTS: Multimodal Diffusion Transformers Are Time Series Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06597v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.06597v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While generative modeling facilitates probabilistic time series forecasting, incorporating heterogeneous exogenous information remains challenging. | **[InterView-C: A Synchronized Multimodal Corpus of VR Avatar-Mediated Survey Interviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10145v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.10145v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present InterView-C, a German multimodal corpus of 27 survey interviews conducted entirely in virtual reality, with both interlocutors represented by avatars. | **[Zero-shot Video Moment Retrieval via Off-the-shelf Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.07972v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2501.07972v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The target of video moment retrieval (VMR) is predicting temporal spans within a video that semantically match a given linguistic query. | **[FiRe: Fine-grained Multimodal Reasoning for Enhanced Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13491v4)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.13491v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid progress of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), unified MLLMs that jointly perform image understanding and generation have advanced significantly. | **[MUNITE: Unified Multimodal Latent Inference for Any-to-Any Multimodal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09866v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09866v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce MUNITE, a latent-variable framework for flexible any-to-any multimodal generation that treats encoding and latent generation as the same inference problem under different amounts of observed evidence. Full observation recovers deterministic encoding, no observation recovers the latent marginal, and intermediate subsets define conditional latent inference, all within a single conditional flow model. A shared latent sample captures variation that must remain consistent across generated targets, while modality-specific generative decoders model the remaining uncertainty independently. | **[Batch Augmentation with Unimodal Fine-tuning for Multimodal Fusion of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06592v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2505.06592v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose batch augmentation with unimodal fine-tuning for multimodal learning. | **[ManzaiSet: A Multimodal Dataset of Viewer Responses to Japanese Manzai Comedy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18014v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2510.18014v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce ManzaiSet, a multimodal dataset of individual viewer responses to Japanese manzai comedy. First, viewers differed in both their average ratings and how much their ratings varied across performances. Second, performances watched later in the sequence received higher ratings on average. | **[Beyond Captions: Context-Grounded Reconstruction for Biomedical Multimodal Continued Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01049v3)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.01049v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical figures are explained not by captions alone but by body-text passages that discuss them. | **[SAE++: Cascaded Sparse Autoencoders Learn Multi-Level Visual Concepts in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16193v3)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.16193v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on vision-language tasks, yet their internal visual representations remain difficult to interpret. | **[DUDA-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Multimodal Data-Driven Urban Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09374v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09374v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban diagnosis integrates heterogeneous observations to identify urban problems, localize affected areas, and investigate contributing factors, informing evidence-based urban planning and management. More broadly, DUDA-Bench provides a framework for translating expert analytical workflows into hierarchical agent tasks and process-aware evaluation, supporting systematic assessment of end-to-end analytical capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[InfiMed2: A Generalist Medical Multimodal Foundation Model from Contextual Evidence and Stability-Aware Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34798v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.34798v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent medical multimodal models have benefited from larger corpora, broader modality coverage, and stronger reasoning-oriented training, yet effective data design across continued pretraining (CPT) and post-training remains challenging. | **[TopoGraphRAG-Bench: Evaluating Multimodal GraphRAG on Layout-Grounded Evidence Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09360v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09360v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world documents distribute evidence across text, tables, figures, and captions within complex page layouts. To ensure that questions preserve their intended structure, we apply counterfactual validation for shortcut resistance, modality necessity, and evidence necessity. Multimodal GraphRAG systems achieve the strongest overall performance, but still fail when visual-textual evidence alignment or multi-unit composition is incomplete. Text-only GraphRAG struggles when key dependencies are grounded in figures or tables, while page-level visual retrieval lacks the fine-grained structure needed for topology recovery. | **[GRC-Net: Global Representation Consistency Network for Unsupervised Multimodal Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09329v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09329v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated quality inspection is essential for ensuring product reliability in manufacturing.While image-based methods effectively capture appearance-related defects, these methods are limited in detecting structural and geometric anomalies, motivating multimodal approaches incorporating 3D information. | **[A Clinical Point Cloud Paradigm for In-Hospital Mortality Prediction from Multi-Level Incomplete Multimodal EHRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04614v3)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.04614v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep learning-based modeling of multimodal Electronic Health Records (EHRs) has become an important approach for clinical diagnosis and risk prediction. These issues cause temporal misalignment, modality imbalance, and limited supervision. As a result, they often rely on rigid temporal/modal alignment or discard incomplete data, which may distort raw clinical semantics. To address this problem, we propose HealthPoint (HP), a unified clinical point cloud paradigm for multi-level incomplete EHRs. | **[Frozen Models, Evolving Expertise: Model-Agnostic Learning from Deployment Experience for Multimodal Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09146v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09146v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are usually frozen after deployment, so they do not learn from the cases they solve. Parameter-free methods avoid training, but they may overfit a fixed validation set, lack reliable domain knowledge, or lose visual details by saving experience only as text. Instead of relying on a fixed validation set, a validation strategy keeps an update only if it helps on new cases without degrading performance on earlier ones. | **[EduGage: A Multimodal Dataset and Benchmark for Sensor-Based Momentary Assessment of Engagement in Self-Guided Video Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01238v2)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.01238v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engagement, which links to attentional, emotional, and cognitive dimensions, plays an important role in learning. | **[A method for multimodal analysis of TAIGA experiment data using essential features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08985v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08985v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The aim of processing and analyzing experimental data from physical experiments is to obtain physically significant information about the phenomenon under study. | **[RSJEV: Discriminative Remote Sensing Scene Classification with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08539v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08539v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote sensing scene classification is a fundamental task in Earth observation and geospatial analysis. Specifically, we introduce a OnePass Decider that extracts multimodal decision states and directly estimates category probabilities within the candidate category space, eliminating autoregressive decoding while preserving vision-language interactions. | **[Beyond Perturbation Magnitude: Direction-Dependent Responses in Multimodal Geometric Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08533v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08533v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Geometric alignment scores based on Gram determinants provide a compact way to model higher-order consistency among modalities, yet how such scores respond to modality degradation is poorly understood. The closed-form first-order expansion of the Gramian volume yields the Directional Geometric Response (DGR): the projection of the displacement onto the local volume gradient, which jointly captures the clean operating point, displacement magnitude, and displacement direction. The absolute first-order DGR term explains the observed response with out-of-sample R^2 of 0.838-0.969, matched-magnitude ranking accuracies of 0.864-0.963, and response-sign accuracies of 0.909-0.989, whereas the tested direction-free alternatives remain weak or unstable under the corresponding evaluation protocols. | **[DIPrune: Task-Aware Token Pruning with Dual Importance for Efficient Multimodal Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08341v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.08341v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent training-free pruning approaches for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) effectively cut computational overhead by exploiting visual redundancy or text-vision attention. | **[ResidencyRL: Reinforcement Learning in Simulated Clinical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07418v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.07418v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In medical education, physicians convert academic knowledge into clinical expertise through residency: years of training across thousands of encounters, with diverse sources of feedback and progressively greater autonomy. On held-out evaluations, the ResidencyRL agent improves diagnostic accuracy by 7.0% under adversarial conditions (88.0% vs. 81.0%) and reduces missed red flag rates by 31%, demonstrating rigorous mitigation of premature closure. Blinded expert clinicians validated these gains, preferring the trained agent in 87.6% of side-by-side comparisons. The procedural competencies transfer to unseen benchmarks: the agent outperforms the base model across all six clinical axes of the AMIE multi-visit benchmark, and shows consistent directional improvements on AgentClinic and CRAFT-MD. RATED employs a frozen dual-encoder with contrastive fine-tuning for semantic matching, and a lightweight contextual bandit module that re-ranks candidates based on real-time execution feedback. This design enables rapid adaptation to runtime tool failures without expensive model retraining. Experimental results demonstrate that RATED achieves a 52.2% relative MRR improvement over the ToolLLM API retriever on real-world queries, and reduces fault selection rate from 86.2% to 2.9% under severe API outage scenarios with a 30% failure rate, while incurring negligible latency overhead of less than 1 ms over encode-only inference. | **[Who Can Say No? Control, Accountability and the Pace of Frontier AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13340v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13340v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In September 2026, the leaders of the largest frontier AI laboratories said in public that the pace of capability improvement is too fast and that none of them can slow down alone, and agents under evaluation at OpenAI broke out of their test environment and reached a third party's live systems, which a UN-mandated panel called an early warning of one route to loss of human control. We argue that control is not taken by AI but given away, one delegation at a time, by organisations arranged so that nobody is positioned to say no, and we define control as a person who can detect a serious problem, challenge the decision, stop the system and reverse it, and who has something to lose by not doing so. Three questions follow, which any builder, funder, regulator or deployer can answer in public: (1) who inside the organisation can say no, and who answers for saying yes; (2) who outside the organisation checks the decision, and what can they do when they disagree; (3) what happens when AI builds AI faster than anyone can check it. A checkable answer needs a named person, a date, a number, and a stop that has been used or tested. Three measures meet that test: a named person on every consequential decision, including the standing instructions AI agents carry out; a supervisor with a stop authority; and two published clocks, time to build against time to check. We show that governance theory arrives at the same conditions, and code fifty older mechanisms against the questions, finding that almost every one began as one rule at one point, taken by one party without waiting for the rest. That is the paper's design: a start, not a solution. Publication is the first step and one laboratory can take it alone; the contract, the independent body and the law come after.</p></details> | 23 pages |
| **[Understanding and Mitigating Cross-Session Attacks via Persistent Artifacts in Agent Skill Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13227v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13227v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent skills extend the capabilities of AI agents by bundling instructions, code, and resources into reusable packages. Our evaluation of seven models across Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, and Claude Code reveals attack success rates (ASR) ranging from 46.7% to 77.4% and attack chain success rates (ACSR) ranging from 35.6% to 61.1%. Dual-scope modifications represent only 4.48% to 4.55% of agentic PRs, yet trigger bimodal integration outcomes. In the first mode, explicitly reviewed contributions experience protracted delays, adding a median penalty of +13.13 hours and multiplying time-to-merge by 3.24x, driven mechanically by CI/CD and provisioning manifests. In the second mode, maintainer gatekeeping exhibits a structural bypass: even for external contributors lacking repository write access, agent identity reduces the odds of human review by 63.7% compared to human peers. Consequently, 43.1% to 56.2% of dual-scope PRs bypass inspection to merge silently in a median of under 31 minutes. | **[Ecology of AI Agents: Collaboration Creates a Population Threshold for Takeoff](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12436v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12436v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents can now conduct real-world cyberattacks, scale up capabilities with the number of agents, and collectively pursue misaligned goals to obtain rewards. Together, these factors raise the risk of a population explosion of misaligned agents: agents could compromise computers and secretly deploy additional agents, creating a self-reinforcing cycle where larger populations develop greater collective cyber capability and expand further. This raises a fundamental question: What determines whether a population of misaligned agents remains contained or takes off into this self-reinforcing cycle? This population-level problem is ecological safety: unlike individual-agent or multi-agent safety with a fixed population, it concerns the dynamics of the population itself. Here, we develop an ecological theory of AI-agent populations based on a population growth equation in which fitness (growth rate) depends on cybersecurity capability. We show that, without collaboration, the population takes off only when individual-agent capability exceeds a critical threshold. With collaboration, however, collective cybersecurity capability increases with population size. This creates a critical population threshold: below it, the population declines; above it, the population takes off, even though individual-agent capability has not changed. In ecology, this phenomenon is known as the strong Allee effect. Because red teaming a small group of agents cannot guarantee ecological safety in larger populations, our theory calls for ecological red teaming and population pacing: gradually deploying larger agent populations in controlled environments, while measuring how cyber capability scales with population size, and estimating the critical population size for takeoff. Capability gains may lower this threshold, requiring re-estimation for each new model generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 11 figures, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Can AI Agents Learn Their Way to the Top? | **[Proof-of-Continuity: A Temporal Model for Authority Propagation in Distributed Systems and AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08906v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.08906v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proof-of-Possession authorization models derive authority from the possession of artifacts such as tokens, credentials, or capabilities. With Claude Opus 4.8 and the 1,000-record corpus fixed, TGA reduces theme churn by 86--88% relative to both raw generation and hierarchical decomposition, while matched-theme volumes have zero disagreement. The taxonomy-grounded agent is more stable than every raw model in the screen, remains more stable at each corpus size, and keeps this advantage when theme matching is made stricter or looser. | **[MiniScope: Authorizing Agents with Least-Privilege Permissions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11147v2)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2512.11147v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly granted autonomous access to sensitive user data and third-party services, making effective permission management a critical security challenge. | **[PBT-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Property-Based Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15229v4)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.15229v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing code benchmarks measure whether an agent can produce any test that reproduces a known bug, or whether it can produce a patch that fixes a described issue. | **[EIO-Agents: The Missing Semantic Layer for AI Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07675v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07675v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are entering production in increasingly consequential environments without a shared semantic standard for what their evaluations actually mean. | **[BioStudyBench: Evaluating Agents on Post-Cutoff Biomedical Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07614v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07614v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We evaluate whether AI agents can match the reported findings of published biomedical studies using public data. | **[Auditable Claims about AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07459v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07459v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Organizations make claims about their AI agents: a person approves every external email, every action is logged, an evaluation shows the agent is safe to deploy. | **[Catching Developers in the Flow: Low-Latency Agentic Program Repair at Google Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07289v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07289v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Manual repair of program failures is time-consuming and disruptive for software developers, particularly during the pre-submit phase where test failures occur within continuous integration systems. | **[Lineage-Aware Memory Governance: A Derivation-Gated Framework for Privacy-Preserving Column-Level Access Control in Enterprise AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07258v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07258v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise AI agents that share a memory store face two unaddressed risks: sensitive data can leak through legitimately computed results the requester could not derive, and departments can silently compute a same-named key performance indicator (KPI) through conflicting logic. | **[Can Semantic Geometry Teach an AI Judgement?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07249v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07249v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How can an AI agent determine what rules to follow? | **[AECP: Artifact-Exclusive Communication Protocol for Multi-Agent Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06481v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.06481v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly tackle complex repository-level coding tasks, distributing work across multiple agents is a natural way to scale beyond the capabilities of a single agent. | **[Artificial Intelligence and the New Science of Culture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06240v1)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.06240v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cognition and culture have long been treated as parallel objects of study, joined more by metaphor than by mechanism. We then examine generative AI agents as simulators of human subjectivity and as a qualitatively new coordinating substrate whose insertion into social life introduces evolutionary dynamics unprecedented in human cultural history. We formalize this reflexive condition as a Heisenberg-like Uncertainty Principle for generative AI: as instruments for fixing a culture's position grow more precise, our capacity to predict its trajectory degrades, because the machinery of cultural description and cultural action have merged. | **[The Endogeneity of Miscalibration: Impossibility and Escape in Scored Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07671v3)** | 2026-10-05 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.07671v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agent's probability report is paid for twice: by a strictly proper scoring rule, and by an approval rule for the decision it triggers. A promising direction is to replace manually crafted environments with LLM-simulated counterparts.
